    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I’m here because I keep having these nights where I eat an amount of food that honestly scares me. I’ll... I'll start with some leftovers after I finish grading papers, and then I just end up going through cereal, crackers, peanut butter, and... basically whatever snacks I packed for my classroom. It's just a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Once I start, I completely lose control. It feels like... well, like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, saying, 'Joan, stop.' But I can't. My hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth anyway, and I just keep going until I'm painfully full.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control and eating a large amount of food happen?\n\nPatient: For the past few months... there has rarely been a week without at least one night where I do this and eat far more than I meant to. And then... well, the next day I always try to make up for it. I'll just skip eating while I'm at school, or I'll push myself through a really long walk or do extra yoga to try and fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to get rid of the calories from these episodes by vomiting, laxatives, or other means?\n\nPatient: No, I don't throw up or take laxatives. Like I mentioned before, I just... I try to handle it by not eating the next day. I'll tell myself I'm not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school. And I'll just push myself physically. I'll stay out on the walking trail for hours, or I'll force myself to do extra yoga, even when I'm completely shaky and exhausted. That's how I try to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to not eat the next day or to exercise extra are intended to prevent yourself from gaining weight after these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. I'm terrified of gaining weight from all the food I just ate. So, like I was saying, after one of those nights, I'll tell myself I'm just not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school. And then I'll stay out on the trail for hours, or I'll do that extra yoga even when I'm completely shaky and exhausted... it's all just to burn it off so I don't put on weight.\n\nDoctor: How often do you engage in these extra physical activities or restrict your eating after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Like I mentioned earlier, it's pretty much every time I have one of those nights. So for the past few months... it's been at least once a week. \n\nWhenever I eat far more than I meant to, the very next day is when I try to make up for it. I'll skip eating at school, or I'll push myself through that long walk or the extra yoga. They basically always happen together.\n\nDoctor: Does your weight or body shape influence how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really does. If I look even a little softer around my stomach, or... if my pants feel tight before a staff meeting, I honestly feel like I’ve failed at everything. It doesn't even matter how well my students are doing or how prepared I am for the day. My weight and shape basically determine my entire worth.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it goes up and down a little bit—maybe a pound or two depending on the week—but... overall, it's stayed pretty much the same. There haven't been any major drops or gains.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your weight or body shape is the most important aspect of your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying a minute ago... it really is. It completely takes over. If I notice I'm looking even a little softer around my stomach, or... if my pants feel tight right before a staff meeting, I feel like a total failure. It honestly doesn't matter how well my students are doing in class, or how prepared I am for the school day. If my weight or shape isn't right, I feel like I've failed at everything. So, yes, it's definitely the biggest part of how I judge myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any guilt or shame after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I haven't really noticed feeling guilty or ashamed right after I eat like that. I usually just feel... I don't know, just really full and tired, mostly. But I wouldn't say I sit there and feel shame over it, no.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself thinking about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to put a number on it. I don't track exactly how many times a day I think about it... it's just kind of always there in the background. Like I was saying before, it's a huge focus for me, so it's on my mind a lot during the day, especially when I'm getting ready for school or if I catch a reflection of myself. But if you're looking for a specific amount of times a day or something like that, I really couldn't say.\n\nDoctor: How often do you criticize yourself for your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how often, exactly. I mean, like I said, it's on my mind a lot, but I wouldn't say I'm constantly standing there actively criticizing myself out loud or anything. It's more just... a general feeling I have about myself throughout the day. So I can't really give you a specific number of times I do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean... I haven't really noticed feeling guilty or ashamed right after I eat like that. I usually just feel... I don't know, just really full and tired, mostly. But I wouldn't say I sit there and feel shame over it, no.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself thinking about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to put a number on it. I don't track exactly how many times a day I think about it... it's just kind of always there in the background. Like I was saying before, it's a huge focus for me, so it's on my mind a lot during the day, especially when I'm getting ready for school or if I catch a reflection of myself. But if you're looking for a specific amount of times a day or something like that, I really couldn't say.\n\nDoctor: How often do you criticize yourself for your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how often, exactly. I mean, like I said, it's on my mind a lot, but I wouldn't say I'm constantly standing there actively criticizing myself out loud or anything. So for the past few months... it's been at least once a week. \n\nWhenever I eat far more than I meant to, the very next day is when I try to make up for it. I'll skip eating at school, or I'll push myself through that long walk or the extra yoga. They basically always happen together.\n\nDoctor: Does your weight or body shape influence how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really does. If I look even a little softer around my stomach, or... if my pants feel tight before a staff meeting, I honestly feel like I’ve failed at everything. It doesn't even matter how well my students are doing or how prepared I am for the day. My weight and shape basically determine my entire worth.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it goes up and down a little bit—maybe a pound or two depending on the week—but... overall, it's stayed pretty much the same. There haven't been any major drops or gains.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your weight or body shape is the most important aspect of your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying a minute ago... it really is. It completely takes over. If I notice I'm looking even a little softer around my stomach, or... if my pants feel tight right before a staff meeting, I feel like a total failure. It honestly doesn't matter how well my students are doing in class, or how prepared I am for the school day. If my weight or shape isn't right, I feel like I've failed at everything. So, yes, it's definitely the biggest part of how I judge myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any guilt or shame after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I haven't really noticed feeling guilty or ashamed right after I eat like that. I usually just feel... I don't know, just really full and tired, mostly. But I wouldn't say I sit there and feel shame over it, no.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself thinking about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to put a number on it. I don't track exactly how many times a day I think about it... it's just kind of always there in the background. Like I was saying before, it's a huge focus for me, so it's on my mind a lot during the day, especially when I'm getting ready for school or if I catch a reflection of myself. But if you're looking for a specific amount of times a day or something like that, I really couldn't say.\n\nDoctor: How often do you criticize yourself for your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how often, exactly. I mean, like I said, it's on my mind a lot, but I wouldn't say I'm constantly standing there actively criticizing myself out loud or anything. It's more just... a general feeling I have about myself throughout the day. So I can't really give you a specific number of times I do it.\n\nDoctor: How many times a day would you say you think something critical about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't give you a number. Like I mentioned, it's just a constant thing in the background for me. I don't keep track of how many times a day I have a critical thought about it. It's just... always kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you criticize yourself for your weight or body shape daily?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely every day. It's just... like, if I look even a little softer around my stomach, or if my work pants feel tight right before a staff meeting, I honestly feel like I've failed at everything. It doesn't even matter how well my students are doing or how prepared I am for my lessons. That feeling just completely takes over.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I’m here because I keep having these nights where I eat an amount of food that honestly scares me. I’ll... I'll start with some leftovers after I finish grading papers, and then I just end up going through cereal, crackers, peanut butter, and... basically whatever snacks I packed for my classroom. It's just a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Once I start, I completely lose control. It feels like... well, like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, saying, 'Joan, stop.' But I can't. My hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth anyway, and I just keep going until I'm painfully full.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control and eating a large amount of food happen?\n\nPatient: For the past few months... there has rarely been a week without at least one night where I do this and eat far more than I meant to. And then... well, the next day I always try to make up for it. I'll just skip eating while I'm at school, or I'll push myself through a really long walk or do extra yoga to try and fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to get rid of the calories from these episodes by vomiting, laxatives, or other means?\n\nPatient: No, I don't throw up or take laxatives. Like I mentioned before, I just... I try to handle it by not eating the next day. I'll tell myself I'm not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school. And I'll just push myself physically. I'll stay out on the walking trail for hours, or I'll force myself to do extra yoga, even when I'm completely shaky and exhausted. That's how I try to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to not eat the next day or to exercise extra are intended to prevent yourself from gaining weight after these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. I'm terrified of gaining weight from all the food I just ate. So, like I was saying, after one of those nights, I'll tell myself I'm just not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school. And then I'll stay out on the trail for hours, or I'll do that extra yoga even when I'm completely shaky and exhausted... it's all just to burn it off so I don't put on weight.\n\nDoctor: How often do you engage in these extra physical activities or restrict your eating after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Like I mentioned earlier, it's pretty much every time I have one of those nights. So for the past few months... it's been at least once a week. \n\nWhenever I eat far more than I meant to, the very next day is when I try to make up for it. I'll skip eating at school, or I'll push myself through that long walk or the extra yoga. They basically always happen together.\n\nDoctor: Does your weight or body shape influence how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really does. If I look even a little softer around my stomach, or... if my pants feel tight before a staff meeting, I honestly feel like I’ve failed at everything. It doesn't even matter how well my students are doing or how prepared I am for the day. My weight and shape basically determine my entire worth.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it goes up and down a little bit—maybe a pound or two depending on the week—but... overall, it's stayed pretty much the same. There haven't been any major drops or gains.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your weight or body shape is the most important aspect of your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying a minute ago... it really is. It completely takes over. If I notice I'm looking even a little softer around my stomach, or... if my pants feel tight right before a staff meeting, I feel like a total failure. It honestly doesn't matter how well my students are doing in class, or how prepared I am for the school day. If my weight or shape isn't right, I feel like I've failed at everything. So, yes, it's definitely the biggest part of how I judge myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any guilt or shame after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I haven't really noticed feeling guilty or ashamed right after I eat like that. I usually just feel... I don't know, just really full and tired, mostly. But I wouldn't say I sit there and feel shame over it, no.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself thinking about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to put a number on it. I don't track exactly how many times a day I think about it... it's just kind of always there in the background. Like I was saying before, it's a huge focus for me, so it's on my mind a lot during the day, especially when I'm getting ready for school or if I catch a reflection of myself. But if you're looking for a specific amount of times a day or something like that, I really couldn't say.\n\nDoctor: How often do you criticize yourself for your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how often, exactly. I mean, like I said, it's on my mind a lot, but I wouldn't say I'm constantly standing there actively criticizing myself out loud or anything. It's more just... a general feeling I have about myself throughout the day. So I can't really give you a specific number of times I do it.\n\nDoctor: How many times a day would you say you think something critical about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't give you a number. Like I mentioned, it's just a constant thing in the background for me. I don't keep track of how many times a day I have a critical thought about it. It's just... always kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you criticize yourself for your weight or body shape daily?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely every day. It's just... like, if I look even a little softer around my stomach, or if my work pants feel tight right before a staff meeting, I honestly feel like I've failed at everything. It doesn't even matter how well my students are doing or how prepared I am for my lessons. That feeling just completely takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you criticize yourself for your weight or body shape multiple times a day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say so. Like I said, I don't really count, but... it's definitely more than once a day. It just pops into my head whenever I catch my reflection or feel my clothes against my skin.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do you have one of these episodes where you eat a large amount of food and feel like you've lost control?\n\nPatient: Um, for the past few months, I'd say... well, there's rarely been a week without at least one night where I do that—where I just eat far more than I meant to. And then... the next day, I always try to make up for it. Like, I'll just not eat anything while I'm at school teaching, or I'll push myself through a really long walk or do extra yoga when I get home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I’m here because I keep having these nights where I eat an amount of food that honestly scares me. I’ll... I'll start with some leftovers after I finish grading papers, and then I just end up going through cereal, crackers, peanut butter, and... basically whatever snacks I packed for my classroom. It's just a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Once I start, I completely lose control. It feels like... well, like the reasonable teacher part of me is just watching from the outside, saying, 'Joan, stop.' But I can't. My hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth anyway, and I just keep going until I'm painfully full.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control and eating a large amount of food happen?\n\nPatient: For the past few months... there has rarely been a week without at least one night where I do this and eat far more than I meant to. And then... well, the next day I always try to make up for it. I'll just skip eating while I'm at school, or I'll push myself through a really long walk or do extra yoga to try and fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to get rid of the calories from these episodes by vomiting, laxatives, or other means?\n\nPatient: No, I don't throw up or take laxatives. Like I mentioned before, I just... I try to handle it by not eating the next day. I'll tell myself I'm not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school. And I'll just push myself physically. I'll stay out on the walking trail for hours, or I'll force myself to do extra yoga, even when I'm completely shaky and exhausted. That's how I try to get rid of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to not eat the next day or to exercise extra are intended to prevent yourself from gaining weight after these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. I'm terrified of gaining weight from all the food I just ate. So, like I was saying, after one of those nights, I'll tell myself I'm just not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school. And then I'll stay out on the trail for hours, or I'll do that extra yoga even when I'm completely shaky and exhausted... it's all just to burn it off so I don't put on weight.\n\nDoctor: How often do you engage in these extra physical activities or restrict your eating after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Like I mentioned earlier, it's pretty much every time I have one of those nights. So for the past few months... it's been at least once a week. \n\nWhenever I eat far more than I meant to, the very next day is when I try to make up for it. I'll skip eating at school, or I'll push myself through that long walk or the extra yoga. They basically always happen together.\n\nDoctor: Does your weight or body shape influence how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really does. If I look even a little softer around my stomach, or... if my pants feel tight before a staff meeting, I honestly feel like I’ve failed at everything. It doesn't even matter how well my students are doing or how prepared I am for the day. My weight and shape basically determine my entire worth.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it goes up and down a little bit—maybe a pound or two depending on the week—but... overall, it's stayed pretty much the same. There haven't been any major drops or gains.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your weight or body shape is the most important aspect of your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying a minute ago... it really is. It completely takes over. If I notice I'm looking even a little softer around my stomach, or... if my pants feel tight right before a staff meeting, I feel like a total failure. It honestly doesn't matter how well my students are doing in class, or how prepared I am for the school day. If my weight or shape isn't right, I feel like I've failed at everything. So, yes, it's definitely the biggest part of how I judge myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any guilt or shame after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I haven't really noticed feeling guilty or ashamed right after I eat like that. I usually just feel... I don't know, just really full and tired, mostly. But I wouldn't say I sit there and feel shame over it, no.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself thinking about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to put a number on it. I don't track exactly how many times a day I think about it... it's just kind of always there in the background. Like I was saying before, it's a huge focus for me, so it's on my mind a lot during the day, especially when I'm getting ready for school or if I catch a reflection of myself. But if you're looking for a specific amount of times a day or something like that, I really couldn't say.\n\nDoctor: How often do you criticize yourself for your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how often, exactly. I mean, like I said, it's on my mind a lot, but I wouldn't say I'm constantly standing there actively criticizing myself out loud or anything. It's more just... a general feeling I have about myself throughout the day. So I can't really give you a specific number of times I do it.\n\nDoctor: How many times a day would you say you think something critical about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't give you a number. Like I mentioned, it's just a constant thing in the background for me. I don't keep track of how many times a day I have a critical thought about it. It's just... always kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you criticize yourself for your weight or body shape daily?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely every day. It's just... like, if I look even a little softer around my stomach, or if my work pants feel tight right before a staff meeting, I honestly feel like I've failed at everything. It doesn't even matter how well my students are doing or how prepared I am for my lessons. That feeling just completely takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you criticize yourself for your weight or body shape multiple times a day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say so. Like I said, I don't really count, but... it's definitely more than once a day. It just pops into my head whenever I catch my reflection or feel my clothes against my skin.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do you have one of these episodes where you eat a large amount of food and feel like you've lost control?\n\nPatient: Um, for the past few months, I'd say... well, there's rarely been a week without at least one night where I do that—where I just eat far more than I meant to. And then... the next day, I always try to make up for it. Like, I'll just not eat anything while I'm at school teaching, or I'll push myself through a really long walk or do extra yoga when I get home.\n\nDoctor: Do you criticize yourself for your weight or body shape immediately after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah, absolutely. As soon as I finish eating, or even while I'm still doing it, the guilt just hits me. I feel disgusted with myself and immediately start thinking about how it's going to make me gain weight or how my stomach is going to look. It's probably when the criticism is at its worst, honestly.\n\nDoctor: How soon after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food do you start criticizing yourself for your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Um, like I just mentioned, it's pretty much immediately. Sometimes it even starts before I'm completely finished eating. But definitely right after, the thoughts are just right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you criticize yourself for your weight or body shape every time you have one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah, every single time. I don't think there's ever been a time where I haven't felt that way right after. It's just a constant reaction.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. I've never actually made myself throw up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
